(iii) If κ = a²/4 and r(0) ≤ a/2, then r (t) ≤ a/2 for all t. (iv) If κ = 0 and r (0 then r (t) = 0 for all t. ₖ Proof (i) Fix θ with a < θ < 1 and put μ_θ = min over0 ≤ x ≤ θ of (1 -x)(x² -a x + κ ). ₖ

Because the discriminant a² -4κ is negative, x² -a x + κ ≥ κ -a²/4 > 0 for every real x, so ₖ ₖ ₖ μ_θ ≥ (1 -θ)(κ -a²/4) > 0. The field is affine and increasing in s with slope κ , so for s ≥ 1 -ₖ ₖ η it is bounded below on [0, θ] by μ_θ -κ η, which is positive for every η < μ_θ/κ . Choose ₖ ₖ such an η, and choose T with s(t) > 1 -η for all t > T. Then dr /dt ≥ μ_θ -κ η > 0 for every t ₖ ₖ > T at which r ≤ θ, so r leaves [0, θ] no later than t = T + θ/(μ_θ -κ η), which is finite; in ₖ ₖ ₖ particular r crosses a in finite time. Above θ, comparison against the field at s = 1 -η, whose ₖ only zero in (a, 1] lies within O(η) of 1, gives r (t) ≥ 1 -O(η) eventually; since η may be ₖ taken as small as desired once T is enlarged accordingly, r → 1. (ii) Since s ≤ 1, the field is ₖ bounded above by the field at s = 1, which is (1 -r )(r ² -a r + κ ) and vanishes at r-; the ₖ ₖ ₖ ₖ solution of the comparison equation from any start in [0, r-] cannot cross the rest point r-, so by the comparison theorem for scalar equations r (t) ≤ r-for all t. (iii) is the same argument ₖ with the double root a/2. (iv) With κ = 0 the stage is uncoupled and 0 is a rest point of its ₖ own law.

Theorem 3 (weakest stage) Let each stage carry its own coupling κ ≥ 0, let the root be held ₖ at 1 at frozen threshold a, and let the downstream chain start collapsed, r(0) = 0 for k ≥ 1. ₖ Put m = min{k : κ ≤ a²/4}, with m = ∞ if no such k exists. (i) Sufficiency: if m = ∞, every ₖ stage crosses a in finite time and the chain converges to E_rec(a). (ii) Necessity under the collapsed start: if m < ∞, stages 1 to m -1 converge to 1; stage m stays at or below [a -√(a² -4κ_m)]/2 < a for all time if 0 < κ_m < a²/4, at or below a/2 if κ_m = a²/4, and at 0 if κ_m = 0; and every stage after m stays at or below a. Hence stage k recovers if and only if min over1 ≤ j ≤ k of κ > a²/4, recovery stops at stage m, and the arrest depth identifies m. ⱼ Proof (i) The first stage has no rest point below 1 when κ₁ > a²/4 (Theorem 2(i)) and converges to 1 from r₁(0) = 0. Lemma 1(i) applied with s = r₁ carries the second stage across in finite time and to 1 whenever κ₂ > a²/4, which reproduces for the third stage the hypothesis the first supplied for the second, and the induction runs down the chain. (ii) Stages 1 to m -1 converge to 1 by the induction in (i). Stage m starts at 0 with a predecessor in [0, 1] tending to 1, so Lemma 1(ii), (iii) or (iv) applies according to whether 0 < κ_m < a²/4, κ_m = a²/4 or κ_m = 0, and keeps it at or below the stated bound, which is below a in every case. For k > m, by induction on k, r ₁(t) ≤ a for all t, so the field of stage k is bounded above by F(x) = x(1 -ₖ₋ x)(x -a) + κ (a -x), which has a rest point at x = a; the solution of dx/dt = F(x) from x(0) = 0 ₖ cannot cross that rest point, and by the comparison theorem r (t) ≤ x(t) ≤ a for all t.

The ₖ characterisation of the recovering stages follows: stage k recovers when every coupling up to k is supercritical, by (i) applied to the first k stages, and does not when some κ with j ≤ k is at ⱼ or below a²/4, since then m ≤ k.

The lemma also shows what the margin costs. At κ = 0.0901, a margin of 10 ⁴ above a₀²/4, ₖ ⁻ the field on [0, 0.9] is bounded below by 7.0 × 10 ⁵ and η must be smaller than 7.8 × 10 ⁴; at ⁻ ⁻ κ = 0.20 the bound is 4.7 × 10 ² and η may be as large as 0.52. Integrating the comparison ₖ ⁻ equation at η = μ_θ/2κ gave crossing times of 627, 82, 29 and 9.3 at κ = 0.0901, 0.095, 0.12 ₖ ₖ and 0.20, in every case inside the bound. A predecessor that merely converges, rather than one held at 1, delays the crossing but cannot prevent it. Bisecting each coupling with the others held at 1.0, from the supracritical excursion applied to the collapsed chain, returned 0.0900000 at every stage, and the arrest depth was the first subcritical stage throughout, including vectors whose minimum sits within 0.001 of the boundary (Fig. 3a). κ_collapse(Q) = (1 -a(Q))² / 4, (6) so that from the recovered start the first stage converges to 0 if κ > κ_collapse and to the surviving high rest point r₁ = [(1 + a) + √((1 -a)² -4κ)]/2 if κ < κ_collapse. Proof With r₀ = 0 the first-stage condition is r₁(1 -r₁)(r₁ -a) -κ r₁ = r₁[(1 -r₁)(r₁ -a) -κ] = 0, whose non-zero roots solve r₁² -(1 + a)r₁ + (a + κ) = 0 with discriminant (1 -a)² -4κ. The bracket (1 -r₁)(r₁ -a) -κ is negative for r₁ above the larger root and between the roots it is positive, so the larger root is stable with basin extending to 1, and the two roots collide when the discriminant vanishes.

For κ above that value the bracket is negative on all of (0, 1] and r₁ decreases to 0.

The criterion is 0.040000 at depleted evidence and 0.180625 at consolidated evidence; bisection returned 0.0400000 and 0.1806250, and at 0.99 κ_collapse the chain settles at (0.82, 0.98, 1.00) and (0.62, 0.92, 0.99) respectively, while at 1.01 κ_collapse it goes to the failed corner. Two consequences follow. The sufficient condition of Saito (2026a) for whole-chain collapse is replaced by an exact one, and the quantity max_x g(x, Q)/x reported there as 0.1806 is (6) at a₁, since max_x (1 -x)(x -a) = (1 -a)²/4 identically. And the two criteria are not symmetric: at depleted evidence κ_collapse = 0.040 lies below κ_c = 0.090, so on (0.040, 0.090) a collapse at the root propagates through the chain while a crossing at the root does not, whereas at consolidated evidence κ_collapse = 0.1806 exceeds κ_c = 0.005625 by a factor of 32. A consolidated system propagates recovery far more readily than collapse.

6 Three coupling regimes, one of them metastable

Because κ_c moves with a(Q), the coupling axis has three regimes rather than two (Fig. 2b).

For κ > a₀²/4 a crossing propagates at once. For κ < a₁²/4 the branch survives at every attainable evidence level. Between the two the system stalls after the crossing and then completes spontaneously: residual availability is small but not zero, so Q creeps upward, a(Q) falls, and the branch ceases to exist when a(Q) drops below 2√κ. Three statements are involved and are kept separate: where the branch ceases to exist, which is algebraic and exact; that a trajectory that stays near the branch reaches that level in finite time, which is a bound under a stated hypothesis; and that the trajectory does stay near the branch and escapes shortly after the fold, which is supported by geometric singular perturbation theory away from the fold and by the numerical integrations through it. Numerically, escape, detected on a different event function from the fold, followed the predicted fold by 0.003% of the annihilation time at κ = 0.05 and 0.11% at κ = 0.08, and by 0.002% with the wide selector. Below the band nothing moved within 5 × 10¹ time units. For ⁰ κ < a₁²/4 the first stage creeps from 0.0050 to 0.0238 at κ = 0.003 as Q rises towards 1. Nor is that profile firmly held: the barrier the first stage must clear is 1.7 × 10 ⁴, and additive noise ⁻ of standard deviation 0.008, under one per cent of the capacity range, took about 470 of 500 paths off the branch within 2 × 10⁴ time units, while 0.002 took none (Fig. 3b). Confinement is a statement about the deterministic field, and the regimes read better as an ordering of timescales than as three fates.

The band is stage-wise as well, and this part is a numerical observation rather than a theorem.

Each stage carries its own locus Q (κ ), but none can cross before its predecessor, so one * ₖ expects stage k to complete when the evidence level first exceeds the running maximum of Q over stages 1 to k; where a stage's own locus lies below that maximum it is released by its * predecessor rather than by its own criterion. Over five coupling vectors the fifteen completions all sat above that running maximum, by 0 to 0.041, in chain order, and the intervals between them were compressed, 0.004% to 9% of the preceding wait, because a stage coming online multiplies C(r) and accelerates Q. On the evidence coordinate the sequence is a staircase; in time it is often a burst, and late completions should not be evenly spaced.

Two statements about C(r) are used in opposite senses, and both are correct because they refer to different timescales. On the branch at κ = 0.003 the exact profile is r = (1.000, 2.377 × 10 ², 4.676 × 10 ⁴, 9.170 × 10 ⁶), so C(r) = 1.02 × 10 ¹ and |dQ/dt| ≤ 2 × 10 ¹²: over an ⁻ ⁻ ⁻ ⁻ ⁰ ⁻ escape interval of a few hundred time units Q and a(Q) change by less than 10 ⁹, so holding ⁻ the threshold fixed over such an interval is an approximation whose error lies below every digit reported for the equilibrium branch, the critical coupling, the identification and the modulation criterion; for the escape times near the fold, where the trajectory is sensitive to the threshold, the agreement with the full system is not inferred from this bound but measured directly (Sect. 7 and Online Resource 1). It is an approximation nonetheless, not an identity: the same residual is bounded away from zero, and over long enough times Q reaches any interior level, which is Proposition 7(ii).

The absolute waiting time inherits the evidence rate and the regularisation of Saito (2026a), ranging from 8.8 × 10⁵ time units at κ = 0.08 with the narrow selector to 3.4 × 10⁸ with the wide one, a factor of 380, and is not a prediction here, whereas the band, its boundaries and the locus are.

7 Spectrum on the branch and the escape exponent Proposition 8 (i) On the forward chain at frozen threshold the capacity Jacobian on the partial branch is lower triangular with diagonal λ = (1 -2r )(r -a) + r (1 -r ) -κ, and ₖ ₖ ₖ ₖ ₖ at the first stage an identity.

Every λ is negative wherever the branch exists, so the partial configuration is ₖ asymptotically stable at every point of the branch and normally hyperbolic away from the fold, where λ₁ vanishes as the square root of the discriminant in (5). (ii) Let κ = a²/4 + δ with δ > 0 and write x = r₁ -a/2. With the root held at 1 the first stage obeys and the time spent in any fixed neighbourhood [-X, X] of the fold satisfies T_X(δ) = ∫ from -X to X of dx / [(1 -a/2 -x)(x² + δ)] = π / [(1 -a/2) √δ] + O(1) as δ → 0 , (10) ⁺ so the passage time through the fold diverges as δ^(-1/2) with leading coefficient π/(1 -a/2).

Proof (i) Stage k receives only from stage k -1, so the Jacobian is lower triangular and its spectrum is its diagonal. At the first stage κ = a r₁ -r₁² by (4), and substituting gives λ₁ = 2r₁ -a -2r₁² + a r₁. With D = √(a² -4κ) and r₁ = (a -D)/2, 2r₁ -a = -D and -2r₁² + a r₁ = r₁(a -2r₁) = r₁D, whence (8). Negativity of λ₁ follows from D > 0 and r₁ < 1, and the downstream λₖ are the slopes of the cubics of Proposition 5 at their low roots, where each cubic crosses from positive to negative. (ii) Substituting r₁ = a/2 + x in (1 -r₁)(r₁² -a r₁ + κ) gives ( 9), since r₁² -a r₁ + a²/4 = x². On [-X, X] the factor 1 -a/2 -x lies between 1 -a/2 -X and 1 -a/2 + X, so T_X(δ) is bracketed by the integrals of 1/[(1 -a/2 X)(x² + δ)], each equal to (2/√δ) ∓ arctan(X/√δ) divided by the constant, and (2/√δ) arctan(X/√δ) = π/√δ -2/X + O(δ); expanding 1/(1 -a/2 -x) about x = 0 and integrating term by term gives the leading coefficient 1/(1a/2), with the odd term integrating to zero and the remainder O(1).

The identity (8) was checked against the numerically differentiated diagonal at three thresholds and ten couplings per threshold, with a largest discrepancy of 1.1 × 10 ¹⁶ (Fig. 3c). ⁻ Normal hyperbolicity away from the fold means that at a small evidence rate the full trajectory tracks the branch to first order in ε by the standard geometric singular perturbation estimate (Fenichel 1979); what that estimate does not cover is the passage through the fold itself. On the grid computed, λ₁ is also the least negative of the three eigenvalues, with a smallest margin of 8.8 × 10 ³ against λ₂; that is a numerical observation on that grid, and only ⁻ (8) is used below. The two headline statements are one expression read twice: the critical coupling is the statement that the discriminant reaches zero, and ( 10) is the statement that it reaches zero linearly in the excess, so the passage time scales as its inverse square root. This is the passage time through a saddle-node ghost (Strogatz and Westervelt 1989; Strogatz 2015).

What is measured numerically is not T_X but the time for the most distal capacity to cross its own unstable root, which adds the downstream crossings to the first-stage passage. The staircase of Fig. 4d shows why the exponent survives that extension at the parameters used: once the first stage has passed the fold, each downstream stage spends most of the interval below its own threshold and then crosses quickly, so the downstream time is a bounded fraction of the first-stage time and the δ^(-1/2) law is inherited.

For the first stage alone, integrating ( 9) from r₁ = 0 to r₁ = a at a = 0.15 gives passage times of 311, 1045 and 3368 at δ = 10 ⁴, 10 ⁵ and 10 ⁶, against π/[(1 -a/2)√δ] = 340, 1074 and 3396: the ratio rises from ⁻ ⁻ ⁻ 0.916 to 0.992 and the difference stays at -28.6 throughout, which is the O(1) term in (10).

Extending the excess down to 10 ⁵, the local slope of log escape time against log excess falls ⁻ monotonically from about 0.65 at an excess of 0.8 to 0.501 at the smallest examined (Fig. 4c); a single regression returns 0.529 over that range and 0.509 below 5% excess, both reporting the O(1) term in (10) rather than the asymptote. The full system gives 0.48 to 0.57, and 0.39 to 0.53 below 5%. One control separates the two effects in that spread: with the evidence rate set to zero the six threshold levels collapse, the fit below 5% excess lying between 0.527 and 0.532 (Online Resource 1, Sect. 1), so the curvature is intrinsic to the fold and the spread is not; at ε = 0.02 the fit falls to 0.386 at Q₀ = 0.5 because the threshold moves while the system waits.

The shape of the trajectory matters more than the exponent. At an excess of 5% the first two downstream capacities crossed their own unstable roots at 0.31 and 0.65 of the escape interval and reached 0.9 within 3.3% of it, so each spends most of the interval below its own threshold and crosses quickly (Fig. 4d). The result is a staircase, and this is the sharpest observable difference between the axes: a root-driven crossing is fast, sharp and complete, a couplingdriven escape slow and stepwise.

Fig. 4 Intermittent delivery, the escape exponent, and the stepwise escape. a Escape from the partial branch under rectangular modulation follows the duty-weighted criterion in 40 of 42 combinations; the exceptions are large amplitude at low duty, where the fast-modulation assumption fails. b Holding the duty-weighted mean at 1.2 κ_c, the modulated escape time tracks the averaged prediction for periods below a few per cent of the escape time and then falls below it, so averaging understates efficacy there, the reverse of a. Diamonds mark points at which the escape completes inside the first elevated phase, where the ratio saturates. c Local slope of log escape time against log excess in the capacity subsystem, converging to 0.501 at the smallest excess examined; the dashed line is 1/2. Inset, exponent fitted below 5% excess in the full system against the prevailing threshold, with the evidence channel on and off. With the channel off the six levels collapse; the depression at ε = 0.02 is therefore evidence feedback and not the approach to the asymptote. d At an excess of 5% the first two downstream capacities cross at 0.31 and 0.65 of the escape interval and reach 0.9 within 3.3% of it in both cases. The third crosses at 1.00 by construction, since the escape time is defined as that crossing

Any repeated treatment raises a parameter for a fraction of the time rather than holding it. The field (1) is linear in κ, so the averaged field is exactly the field at the duty-weighted mean and the criterion is expected rather than discovered (Sanders et al. 2007), though the trajectory error is of the order of the modulation period. Modulating κ as a rectangular wave with duty fraction f from the partial branch, escape occurred when the duty-weighted mean exceeded κ_c and not otherwise, in 40 of 42 cells (Fig. 4a); the two exceptions had f = 0.05 and an elevated coupling at least fourteen times κ_c, where the low phase is long enough to relax back. Sweeping the period at a fixed mean of 1.2 κ_c, the modulated escape time tracked the average to within 1% below a hundredth of the static escape time, then fell to a sixth of it once the period reached that time (Fig. 4b). The criterion therefore holds only where deliveries are frequent compared with the escape they produce.

9 Neither axis requires maintenance after the crossing

At the recovered configuration adjacent differences vanish and the coupling term contributes nothing, so by Proposition 1(i) it is asymptotically stable for every non-negative coupling including zero. A system carried past it by either axis stays recovered when both return to their previous values, so durability after a course is not evidence of lasting change. What changes with sustained occupancy is Q, lowering the threshold from 0.60 towards 0.15 and widening Δ_c(Q) from 0.40 to 0.85. Relapse is then a re-crossing of the same surface at low consolidated evidence, not decay of a treatment effect.

10 Identifying coupling and threshold from the resting profile

The obstacle to testing this is that coupling has never been measured, and inferring it from failure to respond is circular. The first stage breaks the circle.

Proposition 9 On the partial branch with the root at 1, the coupling is determined by the first-stage ratio and the prevailing threshold through κ = a r₁ -r₁², with a = 1 -Δ_c, (11) exactly, whatever the downstream couplings, with error first order in the error of r₁. If in addition the coupling is the same at the first two stages, the threshold itself is determined by the first two resting levels through exactly, whenever the denominator is non-zero, and κ then follows from (11).

The elimination carried out on the stage-wise closed form instead, a = (r₁³ -r₂²)/(r₁² -r₂), is exact only on that closed form and biased on the true branch by the residual of Proposition 4.

Proof (11) is (4) solved for κ, and the dependence on Δ_c is Proposition 3. For (12), the true second-stage condition with r₀ = 1 and common κ is r₂(1 -r₂)(r₂ -a) + κ(r₁ -r₂) = 0; substituting κ from ( 11) and collecting the terms in a gives a[r₁(r₁ -r₂) -r₂(1 -r₂)] = r₁²(r₁ -r₂) -r₂²(1 -r₂). The closed-form elimination uses r₂² -a r₂ + κ r₁ = 0 in place of the true condition, which differs from it by κ r₂(r₁ -1).

On the exact equilibrium (11) returns the coupling to machine precision, and (12) returns the threshold to 0.15 to fourteen digits at κ = 0.003. The closed-form elimination is biased by -9.4% in the threshold and -11.2% in the coupling at the same point before any noise is added (Fig. 2c). The reason the first-stage estimator is nevertheless primary is not bias but conditioning and scope: (12) requires equal coupling at the first two stages, which Theorem 3 gives no reason to assume, and it divides by a difference of small quantities, r₂ being smaller than r₁ by a factor of fifty on the branch, so that a 1% error in r₂ moves the recovered threshold by 5.8% and the recovered coupling by 6.9%, and a 1% error in r₁ moves them by 8.8% and 9.7%, against 0.8% in the coupling for a 1% error in r₁ when the threshold is known

11.1 What the geometry does and does not say about ECT

The results above are statements about the model, and their clinical use needs a representation, stated as a hypothesis rather than derived. We take ECT to act as an excursion on the root and, over a course, as an increase of the effective coupling. A single treatment is a large acute exogenous perturbation, so its representation by u is close to direct; the coupling half is a coarse-grained hypothesis about propagation from one capacity to the next, not an identification with synaptic strength.

Under that representation three clinical regularities follow from the geometry rather than requiring separate accommodations: the response to a graded stimulus is all-or-none (Proposition 3), the shape of the lorazepam challenge (Bush et al. 1996; Sienaert et al. 2014); response after benzodiazepine failure needs only a larger excursion; and conversion can be fast, because beyond the surface the chain converges on the ordinary capacity timescale (Sienaert et al. 2014; Lloyd et al. 2020).

The two axes are two states in which one treatment cannot be doing the same thing, and the geometry turns that into a conditional. If the coarse-grained coupling is diffusive, and if the threshold is not itself altered, then no coupling increase converts a wholly sub-threshold configuration (Theorem 1), so an acute conversion requires an excursion-like component. One half of that conditional can be removed: on the dependency graph the root carries no incoming edge, so no coupling of any form reaches it (Proposition 2). On the modelled partial branch a partial converse holds: the root is already maximal, the excursion axis saturated and the evidence coordinate closed, so a durable change of the resting profile there is a change of coupling. Within the architecture, three things other than a change of coupling could take a patient off the branch: an input driving a downstream capacity above its own unstable root, noise, which the shallow barrier admits (Fig. 3b), and spontaneous completion on the band (Proposition 7). The resting profile separates all three, since only a change in κ moves the resting first stage and keeps it moved (Proposition 9).

11.2 An exploratory comparison with published dose-response data

The architecture makes a prediction that published aggregate data can be compared with, though not test with any power. The excursion axis sets whether a crossing occurs and the coupling axis how far it propagates, so among patients who respond at all the depth of recovery should not depend on the delivered amplitude. A meta-analysis of benzodiazepine treatment in catatonia collected 53 studies and found no significant dose-response relationship in effect size, reading that null as leaving optimal dosing unclear (Bot et al. 2026). We took from it the 21 studies with at least ten patients reporting both response and remission. Across a tenfold range of highest daily dose, from 2 to 20 mg lorazepamequivalent, the fraction of responders reaching remission spanned the whole interval from 0.00 to 1.00 and was uncorrelated with dose (slope on log dose -0.04, P = 0.77; Spearman ρ = -0.06, P = 0.79), as was the response rate itself (P = 0.31) (Fig. 5a). The null is what the separation of the axes predicts, because dose is not the quantity that sets depth; it is also what a comparison between 21 heterogeneous studies would show if it lacked the power to detect a real dependence, and the two readings cannot be separated at this level of aggregation.

The same source tabulates BFCRS scores before and after treatment for ten individually reported patients. Their reductions were 0, 23, 81, 85, 100, 100, 100, 100, 100 and 100 per cent, with none between 23 and 81 per cent, an empty interval of 58 percentage points (Fig. 5b). This is an observation about the distribution of reported improvement and not a demonstration that the acute response to a single treatment is all-or-none: the ten patients differ in the timing of assessment, the duration of treatment and the number of doses, and a bimodal distribution of before-after differences is compatible with graded responses accumulated over different courses. The comparison value of 28 per cent, the share of patients a graded response was taken to place in the 35 to 65 per cent window, was fixed before the values were read, and the exact binomial P = 0.037 for observing none of ten there is reported against that prespecified value and not as a test of a derived null model. Partial responses are also the least publishable outcome, and a fivefold suppression would reproduce the observation (Fig. 5c). Studies reporting partial remission separately (Pelzer et al. 2018) give a median partial share among responders of 20 per cent, range 8 to 68, compatible with arrest at a subcritical stage but not discriminating alone.

11.3 Falsifiable predictions

The account is falsifiable in eight places. Falsifiability does not require that every internal quantity be measured: a model with latent variables is rejected when an observable consequence it constrains comes out otherwise, and no later choice of the unobserved r , Q or ₖ κ can rescue it. What it does require is that the condition of rejection be a consequence of the model and not of an assumption added to it. The eight predictions are therefore sorted by what they rest on. The first group follows from the theorems under a stated correspondence, milestones with capacities and delivered dose or stimulus with a monotone excursion of the root for (1) and ( 6), and recorded population activity with the coupled variables for (8), and each carries a condition of rejection. The second group needs an additional correspondence or a stochastic model that this paper does not contain, and is stated as conditional on it. The third needs an anchored measurement of a latent quantity (Sect. 10).

Consequences of the theorems, with their conditions of rejection. (1) After a single excursion the asymptotic outcome of the root is dichotomous in the value reached (Proposition 3), and the depth to which recovery then propagates is set by coupling, not by the excursion (Theorems 2 and 3). The observable consequence is that among patients who convert, the eventual depth of recovery does not depend on the delivered amplitude; it is this, and not the improvement score at a fixed early time, that the model constrains, since a finite-time score varies smoothly with the input on either side of the threshold. Rejected if, with coupling, threshold and downstream initial state held fixed and with the intervention acting only through the root, eventual depth among converters increases with delivered amplitude, dose or charge; an association between dose and depth across patients who differ in coupling, threshold or starting configuration is not by itself a rejection, which is why the comparison of Sect. 11.2 is exploratory. (6) Arrest, when it occurs, is at a stage of the dependency order with every later stage still down (Theorem 3, with the order of Saito et al. 2026). Rejected if partial recoveries are observed with a later milestone reached while an earlier one is not. (8) The two coupling forms are separated by a sign test rather than by a magnitude: hold two populations at equal activity and ask whether the drive between them vanishes, then lower one and ask whether it reverses; a diffusive coupling does both and an additive drive neither, at any weight. This needs multi-electrode recordings of the kind that already exist, not a clinical scale, and it is the test on which the whole division rests (Sect. 12). Rejected, for the diffusive reading, if the drive between equally active populations does not vanish.

Predictions conditional on a correspondence or model not supplied here. (2) Non-response to a smaller excursion and response to a larger need no patient property beyond amplitude, provided the threshold a(Q) and the mapping from dose to the root excursion do not themselves vary with patient covariates; the model does not exclude that they do, so a covariate that predicts response after conditioning on amplitude is evidence against the model only if it is also shown not to act through the threshold or the mapping. (4) Within the slow class, frequency of delivery and per-session intensity are interchangeable through their dutyweighted mean (Sect. 8); this is a statement about κ(t), and becomes clinical only under an assumed mapping from the schedule of doses to κ(t), which is not derived here. (7) The recovered configuration is stable for every coupling, so relapse is a re-crossing of the switching surface by a perturbation exceeding Δ_c(Q) (Sect. 9) and not the decay of a treatment effect; whether relapse hazard then tracks time in the recovered configuration rather than the number of treatments depends on a model of the perturbations, which this paper does not contain. (3), observable half: three kinetic regimes exist on the coupling axis, fast completion, a stall that completes late unaided, and one that does not (Proposition 7); the existence of unaided late completions is the observable consequence, and the distribution of completion times across a population is not constrained, since it depends on the distribution of couplings. (6), second part: late completions arrive in bursts rather than evenly, which is a numerical observation of Sect. 6 and not a theorem; the milestone latencies already reported (Saito et al. 2026) can be re-analysed for it.

Predictions that need a latent quantity measured. (3), latent half: membership of the three kinetic classes is predicted by the first-stage ratio through a₁²/4 and a₀²/4, which needs an anchored index of the first downstream capacity. (5) On the branch the resting first-stage ratio is unchanged by an intervention acting on the capacity state and changed by one acting on coupling (Proposition 9), which needs the same index.

12 Discussion

Of the two quantities an intervention is allowed to move, with the evidence coordinate closed to input by construction, neither is an alternative to the other: each is powerless exactly where the other is decisive. The criterion κ_c = a²/4 carries the rest, exact because the first stage factorises, applied stage by stage, and, read as the discriminant whose square root is the firststage rate vanishing at the fold, it fixes the passage time through the fold. What is exact and what is not should be kept apart: the first stage, the criterion, the locus, the spectrum and the estimator are exact; the stage-wise closed form downstream is an approximation with residual κ r (r ₁ -1), inherited as a bias by the two-ratio inversion. Every statement about where ₖ ₖ₋ recovery stops is a statement about the collapsed downstream start, since a stage started above its unstable rest point recovers at any coupling (Remark 2). Three statements of Saito (2026a) are settled here: its unclassified coupling range is classified (Sect. 6), its partial branch continues at every downstream coupling (Proposition 5), and its prediction that consolidation alone cannot remove the branch holds only below a₁²/4 (Proposition 7).

We are equally explicit about the clinical bridge. Representing electroconvulsive therapy as an excursion with a coupling component is a hypothesis, asymmetric in its security, and nothing here identifies which physiology realises either axis. One weakness is better named than left implicit: the whole division rests on the coarse-grained coupling being diffusive. The one biological observation appealed to here, an increase in co-firing within cell assemblies under stimulation (Moore et al. 2026), does not settle that in either direction, because a change in co-firing, corrected for firing rate or not, reports the coefficient with which one population acts on another, and such a coefficient occupies the same position in the additive form w and in the diffusive form κ. What separates the two is the sign structure of the sink term, which no reported comparison has tested; that is prediction (8). The two forms also make opposite predictions about the acute state, and prediction (1) separates them there: under diffusive coupling an acute conversion requires an excursion, whereas under an additive drive on a graph with an edge into the root a large enough coupling change converts with no excursion at all.

Several limitations qualify these results. Theorem 1 holds for arbitrary graphs and unequal thresholds but only for diffusive coupling, and everything after the crossing assumes a forward chain, though κ_c and the branch are unchanged from one stage to twelve. Which quantities are externally accessible is an assumption. The identification needs both an anchored index of the first downstream capacity and the threshold prevailing on the branch, no published measure supplies either, and the two state identifications are assumed. The empirical section is a secondary analysis of published aggregate data: the depth comparison is between studies rather than within them, so it carries the usual ecological caveat and is confounded by chronicity, by underlying diagnosis and by the timing of assessment, while the case-level comparison rests on ten patients from case series and is bounded rather than established by its publication-bias sensitivity. The dependency order rests empirically on a single series from one centre, in which a hypothesis-blind re-rating reproduced the order without inversion but read the same records as the primary coding, so a bias in what was recorded is not excluded (Saito et al. 2026); and the proportional recovery rule in stroke warns that apparent ordering can arise from ceiling effects and mathematical coupling (Hope et al. 2019); milestone timing in that series did not track peak severity, the rank correlations lying between 0.11 and 0.38 with none significant (Saito et al. 2026). The boundaries 0.60, 0.090 and 0.0056 inherit the illustrative set of Saito (2026a), model time is not clinical time, and the waiting time moves by 380 with the regularisation, so timing statements are orderings.

The evidence coordinate is closed to external input by construction, and the two quantities an intervention can move act on disjoint regions, so a treatment used both acutely and in the chronic partial state cannot be doing the same thing in each. Electroconvulsive therapy is the case in point. Acutely it is predicted to act as an excursion on the root, u, because no nonnegative diffusive coupling carries a wholly sub-threshold configuration across the switching surface, on any graph and in either direction, and because the root of the dependency graph has no incoming edge for any coupling to use. On the chronic partial branch it is predicted to act as an increase of the effective coupling, κ, because there the root is already maximal, the excursion axis saturated and the evidence coordinate closed. Between the two, one exact criterion, a(Q)²/4, applied stage by stage, sets how far recovery reaches and when each stage completes, and gives the first-stage eigenvalue and the inverse-square-root passage time through the fold.

Both halves of the prediction are conditional on the coupling form and on identifying the two presentations with the two configurations, neither shown here, and the resting first-stage ratio is what would decide them. Published aggregate data are so far consistent with both: the depth of recovery is uncorrelated with dose, and no individually reported patient fell in the intermediate range of improvement, though neither comparison has the power to decide. The division does not rest on the disorder, but it does rest on the model class. Within the class of Sect. 2, bistable capacities on a dependency chain, non-negative diffusive coupling, a threshold lowered by an evidence coordinate that is closed to input, and interventions restricted to the root excursion and the coupling, the maximum principle of Theorem 1 holds on any graph, and the criterion a²/4 is a property of a clamped bistable stage rather than of the five precision domains; the three coupling regimes follow when, in addition, the threshold ranges over [a₁, a₀] under the evidence dynamics of (2).

What the paper adds to the mathematical description of staged recovery is therefore not a law for every cascade but an analytic separation, within an explicit model class, of the action that starts a recovery from the action that propagates it: from a wholly sub-threshold configuration the root excursion is the only action that produces a crossing, and on the partial branch, where the root is saturated and the evidence coordinate closed, a change of coupling is the only action that moves the resting profile. A staged recovery for which the same class and the same three conditions can be argued is a candidate for the same separation, and nothing more is claimed for it here.

Online Resource 1 Catatonia recovery separates two intervention axes that constrain electroconvulsive therapy

Journal of Mathematical Biology

Hiroki Saito Department of Neuropsychiatry, Graduate School of Medicine, Nippon Medical School, Sendagi 1-1-5, Bunkyo-ku, Tokyo 113-8602, Japan; Onda-daini Hospital, Matsudo-shi, Chiba, Japan. E-mail: hiroki.saito.neuro@gmail.com 1 Zero-evidence-rate control for the escape-time exponent

The escape time above the critical coupling diverges as a power of the excess with exponent one half (Sect. 7 of the main text), and two separate effects push a fitted exponent away from that value. The first is pre-asymptotic curvature: at any finite excess the fit still contains the approach to the asymptote, so it exceeds one half over a wide range and falls towards one half as the excess is reduced. The second is feedback through the evidence coordinate: while the system waits on the branch, Q rises, a(Q) falls and the critical coupling moves, so the excess against which the fit is taken is not the excess the system experiences. The first effect is present in the capacity subsystem, which has no evidence channel; the second exists only at a positive evidence rate.

The control separates them by a single change. The full five-dimensional sweep is repeated with ε set to zero and everything else, including the six evidence levels, the starting point at half the critical coupling for the prevailing threshold, the excess grid and the solver settings, held fixed. With the evidence channel off the capacity block depends on the evidence level only through the constant a, and the excess is measured in units of the corresponding κ_c; the asymptotic exponent is then one half at every level, but an exponent fitted over a finite range of excess may still differ between levels, because the scaled first-stage equation retains a dependence on a (below).

Table S1 Exponents of the full system with the evidence channel on and off. Each entry is the negative slope of log escape time against log excess, fitted over the whole grid (0.005 to 0.8) and restricted to excesses at or below 0.05 Q₀ a(Q₀) ε = 0.02, full ε = 0.02, ≤ 0.05 ε = 0, full ε = 0, ≤ 0.05 1.00 0.1500 0.570 0.532 0.570 0.532 0.99 0.1590 0.530 0.456 0.570 0.532 0.90 0.2355 0.501 0.432 0.571 0.532 0.70 0.3795 0.484 0.401 0.572 0.531 0.50 0.4875 0.478 0.386 0.574 0.529 0.00 0.6000 0.516 0.430 0.577 0.527

With the channel off the six levels agree to within 0.007 in the full-range exponent (0.570 to 0.577) and to within 0.005 in the small-excess exponent (0.527 to 0.532), across a threshold range of a factor of four, against a spread of 0.146 in the same column with the channel on. Switching the channel off therefore removes most of the spread between levels; the small spread that remains is not analysed further, and since the first-stage equation in scaled variables, dy/dτ = (1 -ay)(y² -y + K) with r₁ = ay, κ = a²K and τ = at, retains a dependence on a, a finite-range exponent is not expected to be identical across thresholds even where the asymptotic exponent is one half in each case. At Q₀ = 1 the two sweeps agree to every digit reported, because z > 0 on the branch makes the only inflow term proportional to 1 -Q, which vanishes there, so the evidence coordinate is stationary and ε cannot act.

Continuing the control to smaller excess shows the approach to the asymptote over the range computed. At Q₀ = 0.5 the local slope between consecutive excesses is 0.512 at 3.2 × 10 ³, ⁻ 0.508 at 1.4 × 10 ³, 0.505 at 7.1 × 10 ⁴ and 0.504 at 3.2 × 10 ⁴; at Q₀ = 0 the same sequence ⁻ ⁻ ⁻ is 0.510, 0.507, 0.505 and 0.503. Both approach one half from above at the two thresholds examined, which differ by a factor of 1.23. One alternative explanation is excluded arithmetically rather than numerically. Every sweep starts on the exact partial branch at κ = κ_c/2, and the first stage there is r₁ = a(1 -1/√2)/2, while the branch remnant at κ_c is r₁ = a/2. The ratio is 1 -1/√2 = 0.2929 at every threshold, since a cancels. The starting point is therefore the same fraction of the way to the fold at every evidence level, so a difference in the normalised initial position does not account for a spread across levels; a difference in the dynamics through the threshold a is not excluded by this argument.

2 Waiting times to the annihilation locus

On the intermediate band (Proposition 7 of the main text) the time to reach Q (κ) is set by ε * C(r) and by the selector scale. In the full-system integrations it ranges from 8.8 × 10⁵ time units at κ = 0.08 with the narrow selector (s = 0.01) to 3.4 × 10⁸ at the same coupling with the wide one (s = 1), a factor of 380, and to 4.9 × 10⁷ at κ = 0.05 with the narrow selector. Below a₁²/4 the locus does not exist and no waiting time is defined. The existence of the band, its two boundaries a₁²/4 and a₀²/4, the locus Q (κ) and the ordering of the fold and the escape * follow from the geometry and are reported as results in the main text; the absolute waiting time is not a prediction, since it inherits the evidence rate and the regularisation of Saito (2026a) and varies by more than two orders of magnitude between the two selector scales. No clinical timescale is claimed from it.

A.11 Use of a large language model

A large language model (Claude, Anthropic) was used during manuscript preparation to assist with language editing, identification of potential logical gaps, and code review. All mathematical arguments, proofs, numerical results and final wording were independently checked and approved by the author, who takes full responsibility for the work.

A.1 Reduction and integration

Except where stated, analyses were run on the capacity subsystem (1) with the threshold held at a fixed value. The equilibria of that subsystem are exact objects; as a description of the full system over the time windows used, holding the threshold fixed is an approximation, accurate to below every reported digit for the equilibrium quantities on the partial branch, where the joint availability that drives the evidence coordinate is of order 10 ¹ so that Q moves by less ⁻ ⁰ than 10 ⁹ over an escape interval (Sect. 6), while the escape times near the fold were ⁻ measured in the full system as well (Appendix A.7); it is conservative from a collapsed state, where the root lies below threshold and the evidence channel only attenuates. The full fivedimensional system (1)-(2) was used for the intermediate-regime integration and for the confirmatory exponent measurement, with the illustrative parameters of Sect. 2.1 throughout.

Integration used an adaptive stiff solver with relative tolerance 10 ¹ and absolute tolerance ⁻ ⁰ 10 ¹³, with event detection for crossings. ⁻

A.2 Confinement

One hundred systems were drawn from each of four graph families, feed-forward chains, symmetric graphs, purely reverse chains and general directed graphs, on three to six nodes, with weights drawn from a mixture placing mass at zero, 0.5, 3 and 30 times a uniform draw on (0, 1]. The same mixture was used for every family, restricted to the sub-diagonal for feedforward chains, symmetrised for symmetric graphs and transposed into the strict upper triangle for purely reverse chains, so no family was given a narrower weight distribution than another. Every capacity was initialised below a₀ and the system integrated with no external input; recorded quantities were whether any capacity reached the threshold and the largest increase of max r over its initial value. Separately, in the forward chain, root trajectories ₖ ₖ from the same sub-threshold initial condition were computed at κ = 0.6, 3.0 and 30.0 on a common dense grid and compared pointwise. The reverse-coupling counterexample used a bidirectional chain from r = (0.30, 0.90, 0.90, 0.90), at forward weight 0.01 with reverse weight 0.10 and with reverse coupling alone. A further 200 general directed graphs were drawn with a threshold per node from the uniform distribution on [a₁, a₀] and every capacity initialised below the smallest of them. The scope experiments of Sect. 3.3 used the same fourcapacity system started at 0.30 everywhere with no external input: the additive form on the complete graph with S(r) = r and with S sigmoidal of midpoint 0.30 and width 0.05, the critical weight bracketed by bisection on the final maximum; the same start under diffusive coupling at weights 0.1, 1, 10 and 100; the same start under the reference coupling at thresholds a = 0.60, 0.40, 0.30 and 0.25; and the additive drive restricted to the dependency chain (Sect. 3.2) from the same start and from the fully collapsed corner, at weights from 0.01 to 100 and with both forms of S, bracketing the weight at which each stage converts. The collapse criterion (Proposition 6) was obtained by holding the root at zero and bisecting the coupling at which the first stage loses its high equilibrium, at both threshold levels.

A.3 Root pulse and excursion sweep

Pulses were applied as u(t) = A on [0, τ] and zero afterwards, from the collapsed chain at Q = 0, at durations τ = 0.25, 0.5, 1, 2 and 5. For each duration the critical amplitude was bracketed by bisection, and outcomes recorded at 0.8, 0.99, 1.01 and 1.2 times it together with the root value reached at the end of the pulse. A separate sweep set the root directly to a value v with no input, over a grid spanning a₀ and refined near it; v = a₀ lies on the invariant surface and was excluded from the grid rather than integrated. The transition was located by bracketed root-finding at three coupling values; the located boundary agrees with a₀ to twelve digits, which is a check on the solver. The propagation experiment of Sect. 4 placed the root at 0.61 with the rest at zero and integrated to 3 × 10⁴ at κ = 0.6, 0.2, 0.095, 0.085, 0.05 and 0.01.

A.4 Partial branch, critical coupling, identification

The first stage was taken from the factorised quadratic (4), which is exact. Each downstream stage was obtained as the low root of its cubic, bracketed on (0, a) by Proposition 5 and solved to machine precision; the residual of the vector field at that profile was below 10 ¹⁸, ⁻ and a perturbation of 10 ⁴ in every downstream capacity returned to it. The stage-wise closed ⁻ form was retained only to quantify its departure, reported as a relative error against the exact profile and against the analytic residual of Proposition 4. The propagation boundary was located by bracketed root-finding on the coupling at which a supracritical excursion reaches the distal capacity. In the full system the fold was located as a separate non-terminal event at a(Q)² -4κ = 0; because that event function is the discriminant from which the locus ( 7) is derived, the agreement it reports is a check on the solver rather than a test of the formula, and the independent comparison is with the escape event, detected on r₃ -a(Q) = 0. Identification was evaluated on the exact equilibrium, for the first-stage estimator (11) and for the closedform two-ratio inversion, without noise and then under multiplicative lognormal noise of coefficient of variation 1%, 5% and 10%, two thousand draws per level, reporting medians and interquartile ranges.

A.5 Unequal coupling, spectrum and noise

For unequal coupling each stage was given its own κ , and the boundary of a single stage was ₖ bracketed by bisection on that stage with the others held at 1.0, from the same supracritical excursion used for the equal-coupling boundary. The constants of Lemma 1 were evaluated on a grid of 20001 points on [0, 0.9] and the bound on the crossing time checked against the integrated comparison equation at four couplings above a₀²/4. Arrest depth was read from seven coupling vectors as the number of downstream capacities above 0.5 at the end of the integration, and compared with the index of the first stage whose coupling does not exceed a(Q)²/4. Existence of the branch was tested at κ₁ = 0.005625 and 0.00563 with downstream couplings spanning 10 ⁴ to 30, and the critical coupling was located by bisection to 10 ¹⁴ at ⁻ ⁻ chain lengths from one stage to twelve. The spectrum was taken as the diagonal of the capacity Jacobian on the exact branch at three thresholds and ten couplings per threshold and compared with (8). For the noise runs the capacity block was integrated by Euler-Maruyama with additive Wiener increments of standard deviation σ on each capacity, step 0.05, the root held at 1, 500 paths per σ and a horizon of 2 × 10⁴; escape was the first stage crossing its own unstable root, and the barrier was computed from the potential of the first-stage field.

A.6 Intermittent modulation and period sensitivity

The coupling was modulated as a rectangular wave between a base value of 0.003 and an elevated value, with duty fraction f, starting from the exact partial branch. Escape was scored as the most distal capacity crossing the unstable root of its own bistable law. For the criterion grid the period was one fiftieth of the static escape time at a duty-weighted mean of 1.2 κ_c.

For the period sweep the duty fraction and elevated value were fixed so that the dutyweighted mean equalled 1.2 κ_c, and the period was varied from 10 ³ to 5 times that static ⁻ escape time; runs in which the escape completed inside the first elevated phase were flagged, since the ratio saturates there and successive points are not independent.

A.7 Escape-time scaling

Escape times were recorded at excesses from 10 ⁵ to 0.8 above the critical coupling. The ⁻ local slope was computed between consecutive excesses, and regressions were also taken over the full range and restricted to excesses below 5%. Reported escape times are for the most distal capacity crossing the unstable root of its own law, which is the definition of the escape time and not the completion of recovery. The measurement was repeated in the full system at Q = 0, 0.5, 0.7, 0.9, 0.99 and 1, in each case starting from the exact partial branch at half the critical coupling for the prevailing threshold, and then repeated again with the evidence rate set to zero and every other setting left unchanged; that control isolates the contribution of evidence feedback (Online Resource 1, Sect. 1), and it was extended to an excess of 2 × 10 ⁴ ⁻ at Q = 0 and Q = 0.5 to locate the asymptote. The fraction of the escape interval showing no appreciable change was computed at rate thresholds from 0.5% to 20% of the maximum rate and is reported as a range rather than a single value.

A.8 Secondary analysis of published aggregate data

Study-level and case-level values were transcribed from Tables 1 and 2 of Bot et al. (2026) and from Tables 1 and 2

(A1) The selectors χ and χ are locally Lipschitz; χ vanishes on z ₊ ₋ ₊ ≤ 0 and is strictly positive on z > 0, and χ vanishes on z ≥ 0 and is strictly positive on z < 0, ₋ so in particular χ (0) = χ (0) = 0. (A2) ε > 0 and ρ > 0. (A3) The coupling is non-negative, ₊ ₋ and the root carries no incoming coupling. Where a coupling per stage is needed we write κ ₖ for the coupling into stage k. Numerical values use the unfitted illustrative set of Saito (2026a), a₀ = 0.60, a₁ = 0.15, p = 2, ε = 0.02, ρ = 1 and κ = 0.6, with the one-sided regularisation χ±(z) = exp(-s/z²) on its own half-line at s = 0.01 and s = 1, which satisfies (A1). Two objects are kept apart throughout: the capacity subsystem (1) at a frozen threshold a, whose equilibria are exact objects, and the full system (1)-(2), whose finite-time trajectories approximate those equilibria to a precision stated in Sect. 6 and Appendix A.1.

) with u ≡ 0, and let S = {r₀ = a(Q)}. Then: (i)E_rec(a) is asymptotically stable for every κ ≥ 0, including κ = 0, and Ê_rec is hyperbolically asymptotically stable;(ii) E_fail(a) is asymptotically stable for every κ ≥ 0, and Ê_fail is hyperbolically asymptotically stable; (iii) with u ≡ 0, S is invariant for every ε and ρ, its position is independent of ε and ρ, and each of the regions {z > 0} and {z < 0} is forward invariant. Proof (i) At r = (1, 1, 1, 1), g(1, Q) = 0 and every difference r ₁ -r vanishes, so E_rec(a) is ₖ₋ ₖ an equilibrium of (1) for every κ ≥ 0; since C(r) = 1 and z = 1 -a(Q) > 0 there, dQ/dt = ε χ (z)(1 -Q) vanishes only at Q = 1, so Ê_rec is an equilibrium of the full system. ₊ Differentiating g gives ∂g/∂r = (1 -2r)(r -a) + r(1 -r), which at r = 1 equals -(1 -a) < 0. The capacity Jacobian is lower triangular, because stage k receives only from stage k -1, with diagonal entries -(1 -a) -κ , all strictly negative, so E_rec(a) is asymptotically stable. ₖ For the full system, ∂g/∂Q = -r(1 -r)a′(Q) vanishes at r = 1, so the Jacobian at Ê_rec is block lower triangular, with the capacity block and the scalar ∂(dQ/dt)/∂Q = -ε χ (1 -a₁) on the ₊ diagonal; by (A1)-(A2) that scalar is strictly negative, and Ê_rec is hyperbolically asymptotically stable. (ii) At r = 0 the same computation gives ∂g/∂r = -a, the capacity Jacobian is lower triangular with diagonal -a -κ < 0, and E_fail(a) is asymptotically stable. ₖ At Ê_fail, z = -a₀ < 0 and C(r) = 0, so (2) reduces to dQ/dt = -ε ρ χ (z) Q, whose ₋ linearisation at Q = 0 is -ε ρ χ (-a₀), strictly negative by (A1)-(A2); ∂g/∂Q again vanishes at ₋ r = 0, so the Jacobian is block lower triangular and Ê_fail is hyperbolic. (At ρ = 0 that eigenvalue would vanish and {(0, 0, 0, 0, Q)} would be a continuum of equilibria, which is why (A2) requires ρ > 0.) (iii) On S with no external input, g(r₀, Q) = r₀(1 -r₀)(r₀ -a(Q)) = 0, so dr₀/dt = 0, and z = 0 gives χ (z) = χ (z) = 0, so dQ/dt = 0. Hence dz/dt = dr₀/dt -a′(Q) ₊ ₋ dQ/dt = 0 on S for every ε and ρ. Because the field is locally Lipschitz, solutions are unique, so a trajectory in {z > 0} cannot reach {z < 0} without spending time on S, which is impossible; both regions are forward invariant. The defining relation r₀ = a(Q) contains neither ε nor ρ.

The stable rest point r₁-and the unstable rest point r₁+ collide in a saddlenode bifurcation at κ_c(Q) = a(Q)² / 4. (5) (i) If κ > κ_c, then r₁(t) → 1 from every r₁(0) in [0, 1). (ii) If κ < κ_c, then r₁(t) → r₁-from every r₁(0) in [0, r₁+), and r₁(t) → 1 from every r₁(0) in (r₁+, 1); in particular the collapsed start r₁(0) = 0 rests at r₁-< a/2. (iii) If κ = κ_c, the two rest points coincide at a/2, and r₁(t) → a/2 from every r₁(0) in [0, a/2]. For the chain of equal couplings started collapsed, (i) gives complete conversion (Theorem 3) and (ii) gives rest below a at every downstream stage (Proposition 5).

(i) If κ > a²/4 then q > 0 on the real line, so dr₁/dt = (1 -r₁) q(r₁) > 0 on [0, 1) and r₁ increases to 1. (ii) If κ < a²/4 then q < 0 on (r₁-, r₁+) and q > 0 outside, so the scalar flow on [0, 1) has the stable rest point r₁-with basin [0, r₁+) and the unstable rest point r₁+, above which r₁ → 1. (iii) At κ = a²/4, q = (r₁ -a/2)² ≥ 0 with a double root, so r₁ is non-decreasing and converges to a/2 from any start in [0, a/2]. Remark 2 Part (ii) is why the initial condition is part of every statement in this section: a first stage started above r₁+ recovers at subcritical coupling, as it must, since r₁+ < a is the unstable rest point of a bistable law. The claims about where recovery stops are claims about the collapsed start, and they are stated as such. Numerically, at a = 0.60 and κ = 0.01, well below a²/4 = 0.09, the unstable rest point is r₁+ = 0.5828: with every downstream capacity started at 0.59 the first stage converges to 1 and the second arrests at 0.0172, the low rest point of the next stage, while from 0.57, and from the collapsed start, the first stage itself rests at r₁-= 0.0172 (Appendix A.10).

For κ < a₁²/4 the branch exists at every Q in [0, 1]. (ii) Let a trajectory of the full system with u ≡ 0 satisfy, on a time interval [t₀, t₁], r₀ ≥ 1 -δ for some δ < 1 -a₀ and r ≥ c₀ > 0 for k ≥ 1. Then on that interval dQ/dt ≥ c(1 -Q) with c = ε (1 -δ) c₀³ χ_min > 0, ₖ where χ_min is the minimum of χ over [1 -a₀ -δ, 1], so that 1 -Q(t) ≤ (1 -Q(t₀)) e^(-c(t -₊ t₀)); in particular, if the hypothesis holds for as long as Q ≤ Q (κ), the level Q (κ) is reached * * no later than t₀ + (1/c) ln[(1 -Q(t₀))/(1 -Q (κ))]. * Proof (i) By Theorem 2 the first stage has a rest point below a(Q) if and only if κ ≤ a(Q)²/4, and by Proposition 5 the downstream rest points then exist; since a(Q) decreases strictly from a₀ to a₁, the condition a(Q) ≥ 2√κ holds exactly for Q ≤ Q (κ), and (7) is a(Q) = 2√κ solved * for Q. The limits follow from a(0) = a₀ and a(1) = a₁; below a₁²/4 the discriminant is positive for every attainable a. (ii) On the interval, z = r₀ -a(Q) ≥ 1 -δ -a₀ > 0, so χ (z) = 0 and, ₋ since χ is continuous and strictly positive on the compact interval [1 -a₀ -δ, 1] by (A1), ₊ χ (z) ≥ χ_min > 0; C(r) ≥ (1 -δ) c₀³; and (2) gives dQ/dt ≥ c(1 -Q). Integrating the ₊ differential inequality gives the bound, and the reaching time follows by solving 1 Part (ii) is a bound on an evidence coordinate confined to [0, 1), not a divergence: Q approaches 1 only asymptotically, and what is reached in finite time is the interior level Q (κ) < 1. The hypothesis that the trajectory stays near the branch, with every downstream * capacity bounded below by c₀, is what geometric singular perturbation theory supplies away from the fold (Proposition 8; Fenichel 1979): on the branch all capacities are positive (Proposition 5) and the branch is normally hyperbolic (Proposition 8), so for ε small the trajectory tracks it to O(ε) on compact evidence intervals bounded away from Q (κ). The * passage through the fold itself is not covered by that estimate; it is observed numerically.

(

Appendix A.10). (11) needs one stage and no assumption about the others.This is what makes the two axes distinguishable in practice. An intervention acting only on the capacity state leaves the resting profile of a partial branch unchanged; one acting on coupling moves it, by a calculable amount, without the patient recovering. The estimator uses r₁ itself and not a monotone transform, so it needs an index of the first downstream capacity anchored at zero for complete failure and at one for the fully recovered level in the same patient, and Saito (2026c) says why no scale supplies it: a cross-sectional reading does not separate the first two downstream capacities, which recovery timing separates. It also needs the threshold prevailing on the branch, a(Q), which an acute challenge does not read, since a graded challenge probes the collapsed configuration and estimates a₀, whereas the branch sits at consolidated evidence, and the two differ fourfold; (12) would supply it only under equal coupling and with r₂ measured to better than one per cent. At κ = 0.003 the estimator (11) returns 2.19 times the true coupling if a = 0.30 is used and 4.56 times it at a₀, against 12% for a 10% error in the correct threshold. The coupling is therefore structurally identifiable and not operationally measurable.

Online Resource 2 (ESM_2.xlsx). Source data for Figs. 1 to 5, one sheet per panel, written by the same script that draws the figures.

If there are things we can improve, please email us at metacheck@scienceverse.org or leave an issue at github.com/scienceverse/metacheck/issues.
